附录十一 M54出土玉器制作工艺显微痕迹探索

陈启贤 徐广德 何毓灵

商代玉工在前人琢玉工艺技术的基础上持续累积经验，使用的工具虽无不同，但加工手法则较前朝更为细腻。以下选取部分器物就其工艺显微特征进行探索与论述。

管形钻具微痕

M54:328 玉纺轮（彩版六八，1）：侧面观察点放大 40 倍，呈现满布同向不规则晶团状凸脊、长条状凸脊，旋痕圆缓现象（彩版六八，2）。这种现象与笔者在实验室使用老竹管带动解蘸水玉砂从玉材上钻下来的玉芯表面观察点放大 40 倍（彩版六八，4）的现象一致。

实心钻具微痕

M54:327玉龙（彩版六八，5）与M54:368玉龙（彩版六九，1）的观察点皆为隧孔形系孔，上大下小。经放大40倍，可以观察出，孔为换用不同钻具，由粗而细多次钻成（彩版六八，6）。M54:368玉龙的系孔则呈现钻孔时滑刀现象（彩版六九，2）。

片状切割微痕

M54:595 弧形玉饰（彩版六九，3）：观察点放大60倍，呈现切割面粗平满布类平行长条状凸脊与凹槽现象（彩版六九，4）。M54:268玉镶嵌（彩版六九，5）观察点放大40倍亦呈现相同的工艺现象（彩版六九，6）。此现象与实验室使用砂岩类工具直接单向切割玉材所留痕迹相同。

器物边缘微痕

M54:314 玉戚器缘观察点放大 40 倍，可以观察到器缘两边刻意打斜；多面满布砂岩类磨石直接锉磨所形成的不定向短条状凸脊与细凹槽现象（彩版七○，1、2）。

玉琮射部微痕

M54:349 玉琮射部与琮体交接处观察点放大 60 倍，呈现散布砂岩类工具抛磨所形成的细长条状凸脊与细凹槽现象（彩版七〇，3、4）。

器物平面微痕

M54:309 玉戈（彩版七〇，5）榫部观察点放大60倍（彩版七〇，6）与M54:382凤纹玉刀柄部观察点放大40倍，呈现平面粗糙满布平行长条状细凸脊与细凹槽、局部晶团状凸脊与凹洼现象（彩版七一，1、2）。此现象与实验室使用砂岩类磨石直接单向抛磨玉材所形成的痕迹相同。

纹饰工艺微痕

M54:363 玉弭口部观察点一放大 40 倍，呈现沟边粗缓，沟壁沟底粗平满布晶粒状凸点、长条状凸脊与凹槽，断面 U 字形，宽深比 2:1 现象。耳部观察点二放大 40 倍，呈现沟边粗糙，沟壁沟底满布长条状凸脊与凹槽，局部晶粒状凸点，条状凸脊在转弯处相互交织现象。龙纹尾部“双阴为阳”线观察点三放大 60 倍，呈现沟边粗缓，沟壁满布长条状凸脊与凹槽，沟底满布晶粒状凸点，断面 U 字形，宽深比 2:1 现象（彩版七一，3~6）。

M54:388有榫玉兽面角部卷曲部位观察点放大40倍，呈现沟边粗糙，沟壁沟底粗糙满布晶粒状凸点、长条状凸脊与凹槽，断面U字形，宽深比约2:1现象（彩版七二，1、2）。

镶嵌工艺微痕

M54:313 铜柄玉戈（彩版七二，3）镶嵌绿松石片观察点放大 120 倍，呈现铜板曲度圆顺，厚薄一致，绿松石镶嵌紧密，铜板平面遗留抛磨形成的直条痕迹；此痕迹当为绿松石镶嵌后经砂岩类磨石抛磨整平再经抛光所产生的现象。

结论

花园庄 M54 出土玉器中除 150 多件玉管外，较为精美的玉器 50 余件。其特点为器类单一而数量较多、器形纹饰特殊、精致。例如出土了 8 件兽面纹玉牌；出土了安阳地区商墓中未见出土的弦纹玉勒 8 件；不同种类的玉戒 7 件；5 件玉珔与 4 件圆形玉龙；出现商代墓葬中作工最为精致的凤纹玉刀（M54:382）等。玉器制作工艺显微痕迹经归纳后发现，花园庄 M54 出土玉器的制作主要经由锉磨、抛磨、刮蹭三种工艺成器；包括次要等共十二种工艺，说明如下：

① 使用砂岩类石片直接切割玉材者；

如M54:158单孔玉矛、M54:368环形玉龙。
② 使用实心工具带动解玉砂旋钻玉材成凹洞者；
如M54:325玉珥、M54:326三角兽头、M54:327龙形玉玦等。
③ 使用实心工具带动解玉砂旋钻玉材成孔者；
如M54:328纺轮、M54:340玉牙璧、M54:343兽面玉牌等。
④ 使用管形工具带动解玉砂旋钻玉材成孔者；
如M54:314玉咸、M54:320玉咸、M54:328纺轮等。
⑤ 使用薄而锐利石片工具直接刮蹭玉材成阴线者；
如M54:394弧形兽面。
⑥ 使用石核工具直接刮蹭玉材成阴线者；
如M54:325玉弭、M54:326三角兽头、M54:341兽面玉牌等。
⑦ 使用砂岩类磨石直接锉磨器缘者；
如M54:268无孔玉矛、M54:314玉戚、M54:315玉戚等。
⑧ 使用砂岩类磨石直接抛磨器表者；
如M54:159弦纹玉、M54:309榫孔玉戈、M54:325玉饵等。
⑨ 使用砂岩类石片直接抛磨玉材成阴线者；
如M54:315玉戒、M54:316玉管、M54:326三角兽头等。
⑩ 使用砂岩类石片加解玉砂抛磨玉材成阴线者；
如M54:160弦纹玉镯、M54:315玉戒、M54:342兽面玉牌等。
⑪ 使用柔性工具带动蘸水解玉砂镂切玉材成孔（拉丝）者；
如M54:351鹦鹉、M54:353玉鹅、M54:354龙纹刻刀等。
⑫ 石核或砂岩工具加压于转动的环形玉器使旋截成同心圈痕者。
如M54:352凸领玉璧、M54:359玉戒、M54:361凸领玉环等。

编后记

安阳殷墟花园庄东地发掘于1992年至2002年，前几年，因故断断续续发掘了一些零星小墓，从2000年年底起，发掘规模较大，时间也较集中，收获颇丰。特别是M54的发掘，最值得关注。该墓是继著名的妇好墓、郭家庄160号墓之后，在殷墟发现的第三座保存完好的高级贵族墓葬，具有很高的学术价值。发掘刚开始，就受到中国社会科学院考古研究所和夏商周研究室领导的关心和重视。王巍所长、杜金鹏主任、殷玮璋先生以及众多专家亲临现场视察、指导，极大地鼓舞了大家的信心和干劲。安阳工作队在驻地的同事们与所领导调来的科技中心等单位的专家一道同心协力，使M54的发掘取得圆满成功。

发掘工作由徐广德和何毓灵主持，参加发掘的有刘忠伏、岳占伟、崔良生、王浩天、李存信、贾笑冰以及安阳队技工霍廷合、屈光富、张贵生、何保国等。

M54的发掘还得到了河南省文物局、安阳市文化局、殷墟管理处和安阳市武警支队的关心和帮助，使发掘得以顺利完成。对此，我们深表感谢！

本报告由徐广德、何毓灵共同编写。其中徐广德先生编写了第一、第二章第二节；何毓灵编写了第二章第一节及第三、四、五章，并由何毓灵通览了全稿。田野、墓葬摄影由贾笑冰、何毓灵完成。铜器、陶器摄影由马怀民先生承担。玉器摄影由王保平先生承担。铜器、玉器线图均由刘小贞承担。陶器绘图由何海慧承担。各类器物拓片均由何海慧承担。铜器修复由王浩义承担。陶器修复由霍慧敏、王艳丽等承担。

本报告的编写获得中国社会科学院B类重大课题经费的资助。

台湾杨建芳师生古玉研究会会长陈启贤先生不但为M54出土玉器做了制作工艺显微痕迹的观察报告，还对M54出土玉器的拍摄给予了全力支持，并特请陕西省阳陵博物馆王保平先生来专门拍摄。河南省文物研究所姜涛先生也前来协助拍摄。对此，深表诚挚的谢意。

加拿大英属哥伦比亚大学人类学系荆志淳教授为M54出土玉器做了近红外光谱分析报告。日本千叶县立中央博物馆黑住耐二研究员对花园庄东地墓葬出土贝类做了鉴定和研究。对此谨致谢意。

北京大学胡东波先生、杨宪伟先生和考古研究所刘煜女士对花园庄东地墓葬出土青铜器做了X射线透视分析。郑州大学刘新红对M54出土编饶、石磬做了考察及测音。考古研究所科技中心的同事们还协助我们做了多项分析、鉴定工作。刘煜、贾莹、姚青芳等对M54出土青铜器做了金相分析。刘煜对M54和M60出土青铜器做了化学成分分析。白荣金、王浩天对M54出土的篆纹圆盘形铜器做了复原和研究。赵志军、赵春燕对M54出土陶囊内积土做了化学分析和研究。王明辉、杨东亚对M54出土人骨做了鉴定。袁靖、杨梦菲对M54出土狗骨做了鉴定。对以上各位先生一并深表谢意。

在整理发掘资料和编写本报告的过程中得到了郑振香、杨锡璋、刘一曼先生的指导，安阳队全体同事给予了大力支持和帮助。初稿完成后，张长寿先生、郑振香先生、王宇信先生、刘绪先生等仔细审阅，提出许多宝贵意见。我们在此深表谢意。

对M54的发掘和研究是经多学科互相配合来完成的，也可以说，《安阳殷墟花园庄东地商代墓葬》是一项集体的工作，许多专家都为此付出了辛勤的劳动。对此，我们深表敬意。由于水平有限，难免会出现缺点和错误，我们真诚地希望读者们批评、指正。

编者

2007年4月

Abstract

This excavation report contains five chapters and appendices. The first chapter examines the sites and excavations conducted at the Huayuanzhuang Locus East (H Locus East). Chapter Two goes through the details of the archaeological features and paraphernalia recovered at Huayuanzhuang Locus East. Typological and stratigraphic sequences reveal no difference from what has been commonly known as “the four periods” of the late Shang culture at Yinxu. It is noteworthy that new insights into the first period, especially the earliest phase, have resulted from the excavations and are presented here. Chapter Two also focuses on the late Shang tombs in general, excavated at the Huayuanzhuang Locus East. Based on the typology of excavated pottery vessels, the tombs have been divided into several periods. The excavated cemetery data are very significant in that they provide direct evidence for understanding the layout of the religious-cum-residential compounds scattered over an area in the vicinity of the cemetery, which has conventionally been identified as the Shang royal palace and temple area. Chapter Three sheds light on Tomb No. 54 at Huayuanzhuang Locus East. This chapter introduces the location and date of the tomb, and social status of the occupant. This chapter also discusses the significance of Tomb No. 54 revealing ritual practices of the late Shang, periodization of the late Shang culture at Anyang, military organization, manufacturing techniques as evidenced by the bronzes and jades from the tomb, and development of arts and crafts production in general. Chapter Four presents the excavated data of Tomb No. 60, including its spectacular shape and size, orientation, principles of burial goods placement, and so forth. Other significant data are included in the appendices

and include human bones and sacrificed dogs represented at the H Locus East, results of bronze material analysis, results of bronze and stone musical instruments (nao and chimed stones) examination; test results of microtraces left by working on the unearthed jades; test results of soil left inside the burial goods; and the strategy for conserving and restoring the bronze dish-shaped vessel with incised designs. All the information is invaluable data for reconstructing the multi-faceted social life of the late Shang period at Anyang, especially in terms of social productivity, social organization and ritual institution.

Noteworthy issues under discussion in the present volume are as follows.

Firstly, understanding of the Yinxu period I culture is relatively limited, which is especially the case for the earliest phase. This is partly due to the fact that only a small number of archaeological features and artifacts of this period have been found in the past. The discovery of the mid-Shang period city site on the north Huan riverbank (commonly known as the Huanbei Shang city site) enhances the interest in seeking and interpreting the Yinxu period I culture straddling the middle Shang represented by the finds in Huanbei Shang city site and the mature late Shang culture generally identified in the Anyang area. The excavations at H Locus East are significant because they fill this gap by identifying the features and artifacts of this transitional phase stratigraphically. A clear sequence has been identified in square T2: layer 1 $\rightarrow$ layer 2 $\rightarrow$ ash-pit H18 $\rightarrow$ tomb M60 $\rightarrow$ undisturbed soil. Predating ash-pit H18, which contains primarily finds typical of the early Yinxu period II phase, Tomb No. 60 can consequently be dated to the first period of late Shang culture at Anyang. In addition, stylistic comparison of the finds from Tomb M60 with those from other contexts of the Yinxu period I, fixes its date to the early phase of Yinxu period I. The discovery of Tomb No. 60 provides invaluable clues for understanding the characteristics of the late Shang burials of the early phase of Yinxu period I, which have rarely been

found in the past.

Secondly, the H Locus East sites are concentrated in a large area conventionally known as the “palace-cum-temple” area. It is in this sense that all archaeological excavations conducted within this area may be essential to the correct understanding of the layout and ritual significance of the building compound. Outside of the fieldwork in the 1920s and 1930s, only a very limited number of excavations have ever been conducted in this area, and this has inevitably hindered the understanding and interpretation of the sites. Small-scale excavations were carried out between 1992—2002 by the Anyang Work Station of the Institute of Archaeology of the Chinese Academy of Social Sciences. Major achievements include the excavations of multi-period burials, which contribute to the understanding of the mortuary practices, sacrificial institution, and principles of lineage for placement in cemeteries. It is commonly agreed that as the constituent part of the palace-cum-temple area where ritual practices were performed, the cemetery site located in the immediate vicinity should also be viewed as sacred and religiously significant and should be expected to contain only large-scale tombs of high-ranking aristocrats. However, surprisingly, quite numerous small-scale tombs have also been found in this area. Occupants within these tombs are thought probably to have been slaves or commoners. It is unclear why so many low-ranking people are buried here. Who these people were and whether the cemetery data provide evidence of inequality among the tomb occupants is explored. This excavation report provides in detail the excavated data of these small-scale tombs, including primarily size, shape, dating, and burial goods.

Thirdly, Tomb No. 54 remained intact when excavated, yielding altogether 579 burial goods of various kinds, including 200 bronze vessels and 200 jade artifacts. Forty bronze prestige vessels, including nine sets of $gu$ and $jue$ , well represent the high-ranking social status of the tomb occupant. Fifteen human sacrificial victims and fifteen dogs were found in the tomb. The excavation of

Tomb No. 54 reveals the multi-faceted social life of the late Shang period. Metallurgical analysis suggests that the late Shang craftsmen may have deliberately cast bronzes with varying proportions of the alloy constituents. As the second largest jade-yielding tomb after the famous Fuhao tomb, the quality of the two hundred odd refined jade artifacts in Tomb No. 54 reflects the elite privilege in using jades as prestige goods. Examination of the micro-traces on jades unearthed from Tomb No. 54 provides significant evidence for determining where the material originated and how the jades were manufactured and used. Shells, probably used as coins during the late Shang, and plant remains have also been carefully examined. The species of shells may provide important clues as to their place of origin. Similarly, types of plant remains are also essential in determining the diet and agricultural practices of the Shang period local residents. Archaeological analysis of the skeletal remains of the major occupant of Tomb No. 54, suggests that the individual was a male of approximately thirty-five years who had sustained violent injury to parts of his bones which had likely caused his death. Other status-determining indicators include numerous human and dog sacrifices, large numbers of ritual bronze vessels and weapons, particularly the seven axes (yue) symbolizing the military power that had been enjoyed by the deceased. Additional clues also come from the inscriptions on bronze vessels. For example, many of the ritual bronze vessels and weapons are inscribed "Chang". The analysis in this book demonstrates that the male occupant of Tomb No. 54 is most likely to have been a general from the Chang lineage, who, partly due to his achievements on the battlefield, was honored by the Shang royal house by allowing him to be buried in the temple area. The pottery and bronze finds suggest that Tomb No. 54 dates from the late phase of the Yinxu period II, later in date than the Fuhao tomb, corresponding to the reigns of King Zugeng and Zujia. Bronzes found in Tomb No. 54 add new information to understanding the craftsmanship of bronze manufacture during the reigns of the two kings, Zugeng and Zujia, which was little

known in the past.

Finally, joint burials like that seen in Tomb No. 60 have rarely been discovered at Yinxu. In addition, all the funerary goods in Tomb No. 60 had been deliberately damaged. Here, this meaningful ritual practice has been identified for the first time at Yinxu. Tomb No. 60 is relatively early in date, belonging to the first period of Yinxu culture. The discovery of Tomb No. 60 enriches the understanding of the first period of the late Shang culture at Anyang.
